PART VI — Mass Production
अध्याय 14

Mass Production: Science, Economics और Reality

Mass Production: Science, Economics और Reality

Mass Production — quantity नहीं, system है

14.1 — Mass Production की Scientific परिभाषा

Mass Production (औद्योगिक परिधान उत्पादन) = बड़े पैमाने पर, highly standardised garments को, specialised operations के through, continuous और tightly controlled production lines में बनाना — जहाँ economies of scale से per unit cost minimum हो।

History of Mass Production:

Henry Ford ने 1913 में automobile assembly line introduce किया। यही concept Garment Industry में आया — पर slowly।

Frederick Winslow Taylor के Scientific Management principles (1911) ने manufacturing को transform किया। Time study, work study, standardisation — सब Taylor से।

Garment Industry में Mass Production का arrival:

1950s-60s: Sewing operations specialised होने लगे। Production lines शुरू हुईं।

1970s-80s: Export industry boom। Bangladesh, China, India में large factories।

1990s-2000s: Fast fashion। Volume और speed दोनों critical।

Today: Digital integration, compliance, sustainability focus।

Mass Production के 6 लक्षण:

1. High Volume: Minimum 1000 pieces/day एक style।

2. Standardisation: Identical products — zero variation acceptable।

3. Specialised Labour: हर worker एक specific operation में expert।

4. Production Line: Sequential workstations।

5. Process Control: हर stage monitored, data captured।

6. Economies of Scale: Volume से per unit cost घटती है।

14.2 — Economies of Scale: Mass Production का Economics

Economies of Scale = जब production बढ़ता है, per unit cost घटता है।

क्यों होता है:

Fixed Costs का Spreading: Rent, supervisor salary, machine depreciation — ये fixed हैं। ज़्यादा pieces में divide होने पर per piece fixed cost कम।

Learning Curve Effect: ज़्यादा production → operators faster, more efficient।

Material Savings: Bulk buying = better rates।

Setup Cost per Unit: एक setup = ज़्यादा pieces → less per piece।

Numerical Example:

Scenario A: 100 shirts/month

Fixed Cost: ₹20,000

Variable Cost: ₹200/shirt

Total Cost: ₹20,000 + ₹20,000 = ₹40,000

Per shirt: ₹400

Scenario B: 1000 shirts/month

Fixed Cost: ₹20,000 (same)

Variable Cost: ₹190/shirt (bulk material savings)

Total Cost: ₹20,000 + ₹1,90,000 = ₹2,10,000

Per shirt: ₹210

Scenario C: 5000 shirts/month

Fixed Cost: ₹20,000 (still same)

Variable Cost: ₹175/shirt (better bulk, better efficiency)

Total Cost: ₹20,000 + ₹8,75,000 = ₹8,95,000

Per shirt: ₹179

Per shirt cost: ₹400 → ₹210 → ₹179 as volume increases।

This is why large manufacturers can sell at prices small ones cannot match।

Diseconomies of Scale:

बहुत बड़ा होने पर problems भी आते हैं: Management complexity, communication breakdown, quality control harder, labour relations complex।

Optimal Scale exists — कुछ products के लिए medium-size factory most efficient है।

14.3 — Mass Production की Prerequisites

Mass Production में jump करने से पहले ये सब होना चाहिए:

HARD Prerequisites (Non-negotiable):

1. Stable, Large Order Base: Without orders, production line cannot justify. Minimum 3-6 months order visibility।

2. Adequate Working Capital: Raw material advance → production cycle → payment receipt। Typically 2-3 months revenue as working capital needed।

3. Trained Workforce: Untrained operators in mass production = high defects। Training program ready।

4. IE System: SMV for every operation। Line balancing done। Targets set।

5. Quality System: Inline QC. AQL inspection. Defect tracking। Buyer quality standard known।

6. PPC System: Production Planning and Control। Scheduling. Material planning।

7. Compliance: Labour law compliance। Fire safety। Electrical safety। Factory registration।

8. Reliable Supply Chain: Material suppliers who can deliver consistently. Backup suppliers।

SOFT Prerequisites (Highly Recommended):

Management capability: Experienced factory/production manager।

ERP or digital system: For tracking at scale।

Banking relationship: For working capital credit line।

Customer relationships: Repeat buyers।

Multi-skill workers: Coverage for absenteeism।

Without prerequisites:

Mass Production attempt → expensive chaos → financial loss → possible closure।

With prerequisites:

Scale → efficiency → profitability → growth।

Checklist: Score yourself 1-5 on each prerequisite. Score <30: Not ready। 30-40: Partially ready. >40: Ready to scale।

14.4 — Production Line Types

Garment Production Lines — Different Configurations:

TYPE 1: BUNDLE SYSTEM (Traditional)

Operations में bundles pass होते हैं। Operator अपना operation करता है, bundle आगे pass करता है।

Pros: Simple to understand, easy to implement।

Cons: High WIP, quality issues propagate, slow response।

Best for: Standard products, stable orders।

TYPE 2: UNIT PRODUCTION SYSTEM (UPS)

Automated conveyor पर individual garments travel। Operator scan करता है, work करता है, release करता है।

Pros: Low WIP, better tracking, real-time data।

Cons: High investment, complex setup।

Best for: Large export factories, high-value garments।

TYPE 3: MODULE/TEAM SYSTEM

Small teams (4-6 workers) complete entire garment or major section. Each team responsible for quality.

Pros: High quality ownership, flexible, low WIP, workers engaged।

Cons: Multi-skilling required, lower individual speed initially।

Best for: Fast fashion, complex garments, high quality requirement।

TYPE 4: PROGRESSIVE BUNDLE SYSTEM (PBS)

Bundle moves progressively through operations in sequence।

Standard size bundles (10-20 pieces)।

Each operator works on one operation per bundle।

Pros: Good balance of simplicity and efficiency।

Cons: Some WIP buildup possible।

Best for: Medium factories, multiple styles।

CHOOSING THE RIGHT SYSTEM:

Small factory (10-25 workers): Bundle or PBS।

Medium factory (25-100 workers): PBS or Module।

Large factory (100+ workers): UPS or Module।

Export premium: Module or UPS।

Fast fashion: Module।

Standard products: Bundle or PBS।

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
Mass Production = High Volume + Standardisation + Specialised Labour + Economies of Scale। Prerequisites must be in place before attempting. Different production line types for different scales. Per unit cost drops with volume — that's the economic logic.
📝 Key Terms

PART VII — Production Line Engineering
अध्याय 15

SMV, Line Balancing और Productivity

SMV, Line Balancing और Productivity

IE Tools — production को measure और optimize करना

15.1 — SMV: Time का Industrial Measurement

SMV (Standard Minute Value) = एक trained operator द्वारा standard speed और method से, एक operation perform करने में लगा standard time।

SMV का importance:

Production targets set करना।

Operator allocation।

Line balancing।

Costing (labour cost per piece)।

Efficiency measurement।

Incentive calculation।

Delivery planning।

SMV Components:

SMV = Basic Time × (1 + Allowance Factor)

Basic Time = Observed Time × Rating Factor

Rating: Observer की assessment कि worker कितनी fast/slow काम कर रहा है।

100% = Standard pace।

90% = Slightly below standard।

110% = Above standard।

Example Rating:

Observed time: 0.50 min

Worker looks slightly slow: Rating = 90%

Basic Time = 0.50 × 0.90 = 0.45 min

Allowances:

Personal Allowance: Toilet, water — typically 5%

Fatigue Allowance: Repetitive work tiredness — 4-8% depending on work

Contingency Allowance: Machine problems, thread breaks — 2-3%

Total Allowance: 12-15% typical

SMV = 0.45 × (1 + 0.15) = 0.45 × 1.15 = 0.518 min

Practical SMV Study Method:

Step 1: Select operation to study।

Step 2: Observe trained operator working at normal pace।

Step 3: Time 10-20 cycles with stopwatch।

Step 4: Note rating for each observation।

Step 5: Calculate Basic Time (obs time × rating/100)।

Step 6: Average all Basic Times।

Step 7: Add appropriate allowances।

Step 8: = SMV।

SMV Values for Common Operations (Indicative — actual varies):

Operation | SMV Range

Pocket Attach | 0.7 - 1.0 min

Collar Make | 1.8 - 2.5 min

Buttonhole (single) | 0.05 - 0.08 min

Side Seam | 0.6 - 1.0 min

Sleeve Attach | 1.2 - 1.8 min

Shirt (total) | 15 - 25 min

⚠️ SMV values style, machine और operator skill पर depend करते हैं। Always time-study your specific operations।

15.2 — Line Balancing: Theory और Practice

Line Balancing = Production line के सभी workstations पर work को इस तरह distribute करना कि सभी stations पर approximately equal load हो।

Why Balance:

Unbalanced line में:

- कुछ stations overloaded → quality problems, stress

- कुछ stations underloaded → idle time

- Output = slowest station (bottleneck)

Balanced line में:

- हर station approximately equal load

- Minimum idle time

- Maximum output

Key Concepts:

Takt Time = Available time per shift ÷ Target output

= 480 min ÷ 300 pieces = 1.6 min per piece

Cycle Time = Actual time at each workstation।

Goal: Cycle Time ≈ Takt Time at every station।

Line Balancing Procedure:

Step 1: Total SMV calculate करें। Example: Total SMV = 20 min।

Step 2: Target output decide करें। Example: 200 pieces/shift।

Step 3: Takt Time = 480 ÷ 200 = 2.4 min।

Step 4: Minimum stations needed = Total SMV ÷ Takt Time = 20 ÷ 2.4 = 8.33 → 9 stations।

Step 5: Assign operations to stations — each station ≤ Takt Time।

Step 6: Calculate Balancing Efficiency।

Step 7: Adjust assignments to improve efficiency।

Balancing Efficiency:

BE% = (Sum of all operation SMVs) ÷ (No. of stations × Takt Time) × 100

= 20 ÷ (9 × 2.4) × 100 = 20 ÷ 21.6 × 100 = 92.6%

Balancing Loss = 100 - BE% = 7.4%

World class: >90% Balancing Efficiency।

Good: 85-90%।

Average: 75-85%।

Poor: <75%।

Detailed Example — Men's Shirt Line Balancing:

Total SMV: 20 min | Target: 250 pieces/8hr shift | Takt Time: 480÷250 = 1.92 min

Initial Assignment (before balancing):

Station 1: Front Placket (1.8 min) ✓

Station 2: Pocket Make + Attach (0.9+1.0 = 1.9 min) ✓

Station 3: Shoulder Seam (0.75 min) → UNDERLOADED

Station 4: Back Yoke (1.0 min) → combine with S3 = 1.75 ✓

Station 5: Collar Make (2.2 min) → OVERLOADED

Station 6: Collar: Split — operator A: inside seam (1.0), Operator B: topstitch (1.2) ✓+✓

Station 7: Sleeve Attach (1.5 min) ✓

Station 8: Side Seam (0.9 min) → UNDERLOADED

Station 9: Collar Attach (1.9 min) ✓

Station 10: Cuff Make+Attach (0.8+1.2=2.0 min) → SLIGHTLY OVER

Station 11: Cuff: Split — Make (0.8), Attach (1.2) → ✓+✓

Station 12: Button/Hole (2.5 min) → OVERLOADED

Station 13: Button/Hole: 2 operators share (2.5÷2=1.25 each) ✓+✓

Station 14: Hem + Trim (0.8+0.6=1.4 min) ✓

After balancing: 14 stations, BE = 20÷(14×1.92) = 74.4% → needs more optimization।

15.3 — Productivity Improvement: Practical Tools

Productivity = Output ÷ Input।

In Garment Industry:

Labour Productivity = Pieces produced ÷ Total person-hours worked।

Line Efficiency = (Actual output × SMV) ÷ (Operators × Available time) × 100।

Efficiency Improvement Strategies:

Strategy 1: Method Improvement

Current method: Operator walks to pick up components (20 steps)।

Improved method: Component bin at workstation (0 steps)।

Time saving: 5 steps each way × 2 sec = 20 sec per piece।

For 250 pieces/day: 83 minutes saved → 83 min ÷ 20 SMV = 4 extra pieces/day।

Strategy 2: Work Aid (Fixture) Introduction

Without guide: Pocket attach SMV = 1.2 min।

With pocket setter guide: SMV = 0.8 min।

Saving: 0.4 min × 250 pieces = 100 min/day = 5 extra pieces।

Strategy 3: 5S Implementation

Sort: Remove unnecessary items from workstation।

Set in Order: Everything has a place, everything in its place।

Shine: Clean workstation, clean machine।

Standardise: Standard layouts for all similar workstations।

Sustain: Daily checks, discipline।

5S Impact: Studies show 10-15% productivity improvement in 3 months।

Strategy 4: Incentive System

Base target: 250 pieces/day।

Base wage: ₹400/day।

Incentive: ₹1 per piece above target।

Worker achieves 280 pieces: ₹400 + ₹30 = ₹430।

Worker motivation to exceed target।

Design carefully: Base wage must cover basic needs। Incentive achievable but challenging।

Strategy 5: Multi-skilling

Workers know multiple operations।

When line gets imbalanced → redeploy workers।

When someone absent → others cover।

Result: More flexible, more efficient।

Multi-skill training: 1-2 extra operations per month। Skill matrix updated।

Strategy 6: Proper Machine Maintenance

Downtime = lost production।

1 hour downtime × 250 pieces ÷ 480 min = 31 pieces lost।

31 × ₹50 contribution = ₹1,550 loss। Per machine per downtime।

Preventive maintenance investment: ₹500-1000/month per machine।

Savings: Multiple downtimes prevented.

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
SMV = measured time per operation। Line Balancing = equal work distribution across stations। Target Balancing Efficiency >85%. Productivity improvement: Method, fixtures, 5S, incentive, multi-skilling, maintenance। Always data-driven.

PART VIII — Machinery
अध्याय 16

Complete Machinery Guide

Complete Machinery Guide

सही machine — सही operation — सही investment

16.1 — Machine Investment Decision Framework

Machine खरीदने से पहले 6 questions:

1. क्या मुझे यह machine चाहिए?

Production में यह operation है? Quantity enough to justify? Manual alternative?

2. कितनी machine चाहिए?

SMV × target ÷ available minutes = operators needed।

1 operator per machine (generally)।

3. New या Second-hand?

New: Higher cost, warranty, latest technology।

Second-hand: 40-60% cost, no warranty, condition unknown।

Rule: For critical operations (quality-sensitive), prefer new।

4. कौन सा brand?

Japanese (Juki, Brother, Pegasus): Premium quality, longer life।

Chinese (Jack, Bruce, Siruba): Lower cost, improving quality।

Mixed strategy: Japanese for critical, Chinese for secondary।

5. Maintenance facility available?

Local mechanic जानता है brand को? Spare parts available locally?

6. ROI कब होगा?

Machine cost ÷ daily contribution (pieces × margin/piece) = payback days।

Example: ₹30,000 machine, 200 pieces/day, ₹5 contribution = 30 days payback।

Machine Categories in Garment:

Category A — Essential (Every unit needs):

SNLS, Overlock, Iron/Steam press, Scissors/tools।

Category B — Important (Based on product):

Buttonhole, Button attach, Coverstitch, Bartack।

Category C — Advanced (Based on volume):

Form press, Automatic cutting, ERP-connected machines।

Category D — Specialized:

Feed-off-the-arm, Flatlock, Multi-needle।

16.2 — Complete Machine Reference Guide

SEWING MACHINES:

1. SINGLE NEEDLE LOCKSTITCH (SNLS):

Applications: All basic seams, topstitch, hem।

Speed: 3500-5500 RPM।

Variants: Cylinder bed (for tubular), Flat bed (standard)।

Brands: Juki DDL, Brother, Pegasus।

Price Range (Indicative): ₹15,000-₹40,000 new।

2. DOUBLE NEEDLE LOCKSTITCH (DNLS):

Applications: Parallel topstitch, belt loops, waistband।

2 needles, 2 upper threads, 1 bobbin।

Speed: 3000-4500 RPM।

3. OVERLOCK (3, 4, 5 thread):

Applications: Edge finish, seam + finish।

Speed: 6000-8000 SPM।

Price: ₹20,000-₹50,000 new।

4. SAFETY STITCH (4-thread):

Overlock + lockstitch together।

Strongest seam + finish।

Workwear, outdoor।

5. COVERSTITCH:

Applications: Hem (T-shirt, sportswear)।

2-3 needles, top decorative stitch + looper underside।

Speed: 4000-6000 SPM।

6. CHAIN STITCH:

Applications: Waistband, crotch (jeans), basting।

Single thread। Easy to unpick।

7. BUTTONHOLE:

Types: Keyhole (jacket), Straight (shirt)।

Auto cycle: Stitch, cut, stitch।

Speed: 600-1200 holes/hour।

Price: ₹40,000-₹1,50,000।

8. BUTTON ATTACH:

4-hole, 2-hole, shank buttons।

Speed: 500-1000 buttons/hour।

9. BARTACK:

Short, dense reinforcement stitches।

Pocket corners, belt loops, zipper ends।

10. FEED-OFF-THE-ARM (FOTA):

Cylinder arm — tubular sewing।

Jeans inseam, tubular sleeves।

11. FLATLOCK:

Flat seam — no bulk।

Sportswear, underwear।

12. BLIND STITCH (Blindhem):

Invisible hem।

Trouser bottom, skirt hem।

13. KANSAI/MULTI-NEEDLE:

2-4 parallel needles।

Decorative stitching, waistband।

CUTTING MACHINES:

14. STRAIGHT KNIFE:

Most common cutting machine।

6-12 inch vertical oscillating blade।

Good for: Straight cuts, gentle curves।

Height capacity: 10-15 cm।

Brands: Eastman, Hoffman।

15. ROUND KNIFE:

Circular blade।

Good for: Straight lines, lightweight fabric।

Limitation: Cannot cut curves well।

16. BAND KNIFE:

Fixed blade, fabric moves।

Excellent for: Complex curves, re-cutting।

Essential for export factories।

17. AUTOMATIC CUTTING SYSTEM:

CNC-controlled।

Follows digital marker exactly।

Investment: ₹15-50 Lakh+।

ROI: Justified at 500+ pieces/day।

FINISHING EQUIPMENT:

18. STEAM IRON (Industrial):

Steam generator connected।

Fast, consistent steam।

Every unit needs।

19. FLAT PRESS:

Fixed heated plate + pressing pad।

For collar, cuff, panel pressing।

20. TUNNEL FINISHER:

Steam + air on conveyor।

No contact — wrinkle removal।

Large export factories।

21. FORM PRESS (Shirt):

Full shirt on inflatable form।

30-45 seconds full press।

Medium to large factories।

22. STEAM BOILER:

Central steam supply।

Regular inspection required (statutory)।

16.3 — Machine Maintenance: Complete System

Machine Maintenance Philosophy:

Reactive Maintenance (Break-Fix): Machine breaks → fix it। Highest cost, most downtime।

Preventive Maintenance (PM): Schedule-based maintenance before breakdown। Best for garment industry।

Predictive Maintenance: Data-based prediction। Advanced। Large factories।

Preventive Maintenance Schedule:

DAILY (Operator responsibility — 10 min):

Before start:

• External clean: Body, table।

• Throat plate and feed dog: Lint removed।

• Bobbin area: Clean and oil।

• Needle: Check for bending, replace if needed।

• Thread path: Check all guides।

• Oil: As specified in machine manual।

During work:

• Unusual noise? → call mechanic।

• Thread breaking? → address immediately।

• Stitch quality? → check regularly।

WEEKLY (Mechanic — 30-45 min/machine):

• Deep clean: All accessible areas।

• Oil: All specified points।

• Thread tensions: Test and calibrate।

• Stitch length: Verify accuracy।

• Presser foot pressure: Check।

• Feed dog: Clean and height check।

• Belt: Condition and tension।

• Hook/shuttle: Clean and lubricate।

MONTHLY (Senior Mechanic — 1-2 hours/machine):

• Full disassembly of cover plates।

• Hook timing: Verify and adjust।

• Needle bar timing: Check।

• Feed timing: Check।

• Bearings: Check for wear।

• All screws: Tighten।

• Motor: Check and clean।

• Safety check: All guards in place।

Machine Downtime Tracking:

Machine # | Date | Start | End | Duration | Reason | Fixed By | Cost

001 | 15/11 | 10:30 | 11:15 | 45 min | Timing off | Ram | ₹0

Monthly summary: Total downtime hours per machine। Identify problem machines।

Target: < 2% downtime (less than 10 min/shift per machine)।

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
Machine investment decision: need, quantity, new/used, brand, maintenance, ROI। Complete guide: 22 machine types from SNLS to form press। PM schedule: Daily (operator), Weekly, Monthly (mechanic). Target: <2% downtime.

PART IX — Industrial Engineering
अध्याय 17

Work Study, Lean और Kaizen

Work Study, Lean और Kaizen

IE — Science of making work better, faster and cheaper

17.1 — Work Study: Manufacturing का Science

Work Study = Method Study + Work Measurement (Time Study)।

Work Study का Goal: सबसे efficient method ढूँढना और उसे standardise करना।

METHOD STUDY:

Select: कौन सा operation study करना है? Highest cost? Most defects? Biggest bottleneck?

Record: Current method को document करें। Flow chart, string diagram।

Examine: ECRS Model:

- Eliminate: क्या यह operation ज़रूरी है? अगर नहीं → eliminate।

- Combine: दो operations को एक में combine कर सकते हैं?

- Rearrange: Order change से efficiency बढ़ेगी?

- Simplify: Complex steps को simplify करें।

Develop: Better method design करें।

Install: Workers को train करें।

Maintain: SOP में document करें, enforce करें।

Method Study Example:

Current: Operator पहले component को table से उठाता है (2 steps, 1.5 sec), फिर machine पर रखता है, position करता है (2 sec), फिर sews।

Observation: Component picking में unnecessary movement।

Improved: Component bin को machine के right side पर exact position पर रखें। Operator reaches (0 steps, 0.5 sec), places, sews।

Time saving: 2.5 sec per piece।

For 300 pieces/day: 750 sec = 12.5 min।

Extra pieces: 12.5 min ÷ (operation time 0.9 min) = ~14 extra pieces/day।

WORK MEASUREMENT (Time Study):

Stopwatch Time Study: Most common।

Predetermined Motion Time Systems (PMTS): SAM, MTM — based on basic motion times।

Work Sampling: Statistical sampling of activities।

17.2 — Lean Manufacturing: 7 Wastes को Eliminate करें

Lean Manufacturing का Core Principle: Value add करें, waste eliminate करें।

Value: Customer जिसके लिए pay करने को तैयार है।

Waste (Muda): कोई भी activity जो value नहीं add करती।

7 Wastes (TIMWOOD):

T — Transportation (अनावश्यक Material Movement):

Garment example: Cutting section से sewing section 3 floors ऊपर है। Every bundle carrier करना = waste।

Solution: Layout redesign — cutting adjacent to sewing।

I — Inventory (Excess):

Garment example: 2 weeks का WIP sewing में। ₹5 Lakh blocked।

Solution: Pull system, smaller batches, better planning।

M — Motion (अनावश्यक Movement):

Garment example: Operator को thread लेने के लिए हर 30 minutes में store जाना।

Solution: Thread supply to each machine। Thread boy।

W — Waiting:

Garment example: Sewing operators wait for cutting to catch up।

Solution: Better coordination between sections। Buffer।

O — Overproduction:

Garment example: Producing more than order — "just in case"।

Solution: Produce exactly what's ordered।

O — Over-processing:

Garment example: Pressing garment 3 times when once is enough।

Solution: Define standard pressing steps।

D — Defects:

Garment example: 15 DHU = 15 rework pieces per 100।

Solution: Inline QC, operator training, root cause analysis।

8th Waste — Non-Utilized Talent:

People's ideas and skills not used।

Solution: Suggestion system, Kaizen teams।

Lean Implementation in Small Factory:

Week 1-2: 5S implementation।

Week 3-4: Value Stream Mapping।

Month 2: Waste identification (all 7 types)।

Month 3: Top 2-3 waste elimination projects।

Month 4+: Sustain and continue।

17.3 — Kaizen: Continuous Improvement Culture

Kaizen (改善) = Kai (change) + Zen (good) = Continuous Improvement।

Kaizen Philosophy: हर व्यक्ति हर दिन कुछ छोटा improve कर सकता है। Small improvements accumulate → significant results।

3 Types of Kaizen:

1. Daily Kaizen: Individual level। Operator अपने workstation को improve करता है।

"Thread reel को इस angle पर रखने से thread less breaks" — small, immediate.

2. Project Kaizen (Blitz): Team 1-3 days में एक focused problem solve करती है।

Example: 3-day event to reduce changeover time from 4 hours to 1 hour।

3. System Kaizen: Large-scale process redesign। Management level।

Kaizen Principles:

Go to gemba (work floor)। See the actual problem।

5 Why Analysis: Why → Why → Why → Why → Why = Root Cause।

Small teams: 3-5 people, diverse roles।

No blame culture: Focus on process, not people।

Document and share learnings।

5-Why Example:

Problem: High rework rate on collar attach।

Why 1: Collar position inconsistent। Why?

Why 2: No positioning guide used। Why?

Why 3: Guide was there but workers stopped using it। Why?

Why 4: Guide slipped and became inaccurate। Why?

Why 5: Guide not fixed to table properly।

Root Cause: Guide mounting issue।

Solution: Fix guide properly → consistent collar position।

Kaizen Board:

Visual board in factory:

What we improved | Before | After | Who did it | Date

Recognizing kaizen contributions motivates further improvement।

PDCA Cycle:

Plan: What improvement to make?

Do: Implement।

Check: Measure results।

Act: If good — standardise। If not — go back to Plan।

Kaizen Results (Industry Examples):

Factory A: 50 small kaizens in 6 months → 23% efficiency improvement।

Factory B: Changeover time kaizen → 3 hours to 45 minutes।

Factory C: Pocket attach fixture kaizen → 20% faster।

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
Work Study = Method Study + Time Study. ECRS model for method improvement. Lean = 7 wastes elimination. Kaizen = continuous small improvements. PDCA cycle. 5-Why for root cause. Culture of improvement > one-time initiative.

PART X — Quality Control
अध्याय 18

Complete Quality Management System

Complete Quality Management System

Quality — पहले build करें, फिर inspect करें

18.1 — Quality: Comprehensive Understanding

Quality की definition Garment Industry में:

"Garment जो buyer's specifications को, consistently, first time right, deliver करे — while meeting cost and delivery requirements।"

Quality के 4 Dimensions in Garments:

1. Fit Quality: Right measurements, proper ease, correct silhouette।

2. Construction Quality: Strong seams, proper stitching, no workmanship defects।

3. Aesthetic Quality: Symmetry, clean appearance, proper pressing।

4. Performance Quality: Durability, color fastness, shrinkage resistance after washing।

Quality Culture vs Quality Inspection:

Quality Inspection (Old approach): Make garment → inspect at end → reject bad ones।

Problems: Wasteful (bad garments already made), expensive, reactive।

Quality Culture (Modern approach): Build quality into every step।

Operator responsible for quality। Check before passing to next। Prevention > detection।

Cost of Quality:

Prevention costs: Training, SOPs, fixtures, quality planning। Cheapest।

Appraisal costs: Inspection, testing। Moderate cost।

Internal failure: Rework, scrap। Expensive।

External failure: Customer rejection, returns, reputation damage। Most expensive।

Invest in prevention → reduce all other costs।

Zero Defect Goal:

Phillip Crosby's concept: "Quality is free" — cost of non-quality > cost of quality।

Zero defect doesn't mean literally zero — means "right first time" mindset।

18.2 — Multi-Level Quality System

A comprehensive quality system has multiple layers:

Level 1: Pre-production Quality

Sample approval process। PP meeting with buyer। Pre-production checklist।

Ensure: Right materials, right methods, right machines BEFORE production।

Level 2: In-process Quality (Inline QC)

During production: Catch problems immediately।

Inline QC System:

• First piece check: Before batch starts — supervisor checks first complete piece।

• Operator self-check: Every 5th-10th piece।

• Buddy check: Next operator checks incoming work।

• Roving QC: QC checker moves through line, random checks।

• 10-piece check system: Every bundle of 10, check all।

Action on finding defect:

1 defect in 10: Warning, continue monitoring।

2 defects in 10: Stop that operation, investigate।

3+ defects in 10: Stop production, corrective action।

Level 3: End-line Quality

After sewing, before finishing।

Complete garment check।

Focus: Measurements, construction, workmanship।

Level 4: Final Inspection

Complete finished garment।

AQL-based sampling।

Buyer specification compliance।

Level 5: Pre-shipment Inspection

Before dispatch।

Sometimes done by buyer's inspector or 3rd party (SGS, Bureau Veritas)।

Final gate before export/delivery।

18.3 — AQL System: Statistical Quality Inspection

AQL (Acceptable Quality Level) = Maximum defect percentage considered acceptable।

Industry Standard AQL Values:

AQL 1.0: For critical items (safety)। Strict।

AQL 2.5: Common for major defects in garments।

AQL 4.0: Common for minor defects।

AQL 6.5: Used by some mass market buyers for minor।

How AQL Works:

Step 1: Know your lot size (total pieces in inspection lot)।

Step 2: Look up sample size in AQL table (based on Inspection Level II, normal)।

Step 3: Find acceptance number (Ac) and rejection number (Re) for chosen AQL।

Step 4: Random sample उतने pieces।

Step 5: Count defects।

Step 6: Defects ≤ Ac: LOT ACCEPTED। Defects ≥ Re: LOT REJECTED।

AQL 2.5 Reference Table (Approximate):

Lot Size | Sample Size | Accept (Ac) | Reject (Re)

2 - 8 | 2 | 0 | 1

9 - 15 | 3 | 0 | 1

16 - 25 | 5 | 0 | 1

26 - 50 | 8 | 0 | 1

51 - 90 | 13 | 1 | 2

91 - 150 | 20 | 1 | 2

151 - 280 | 32 | 2 | 3

281 - 500 | 50 | 3 | 4

501 - 1200 | 80 | 5 | 6

1201 - 3200 | 125 | 7 | 8

3201 - 10000 | 200 | 10 | 11

⚠️ This is a simplified reference. Use official ISO 2859 or ANSI/ASQ Z1.4 tables।

Defect Classification:

CRITICAL: Immediate safety risk or legal non-compliance।

Examples: Exposed sharp wire, incorrect care label (fire hazard), prohibited substance।

Action: ALWAYS REJECT. Zero tolerance।

MAJOR: Significantly affects appearance or function। Buyer will reject।

Examples: Wrong size, broken stitch at stress point, wrong color, significant measurement deviation।

Action: Reject lot unless buyer gives special concession।

MINOR: Slight flaw, doesn't affect function।

Examples: Small thread end, slight soil (within spec), minor stitch irregularity।

Action: May be acceptable within AQL limits।

18.4 — Defect Analysis और Root Cause

Common Garment Defects Reference:

SEWING DEFECTS:

Broken stitch: SMV incorrect timing, blunt needle, thread quality।

Skipped stitch: Bent needle, wrong needle type, timing।

Open seam: Insufficient seam allowance, poor sewing।

Puckering: Wrong tension, wrong stitch length, slippery fabric।

Uneven stitch: Machine problem, inconsistent pressure।

Run-off stitch: Operator error, no guide।

Needle hole (snag): Wrong needle for fabric, burr on needle।

MEASUREMENT DEFECTS:

Wrong size: Pattern error, cutting error, wrong bundle।

Uneven hem: Inconsistent folding or sewing।

Collar not centered: Attachment error।

Pocket misplaced: Marking error, no positioning guide।

FABRIC DEFECTS (passed through):

Holes, tears: Not caught in fabric inspection।

Stains: Material handling, storage।

Shade variation: Bundle mix-up, no shade numbering。

FINISHING DEFECTS:

Press marks: Wrong temperature, wrong technique।

Shrinkage: Pre-shrink not done।

Poor fold: Inexperienced packing।

Root Cause Analysis (Pareto + 5-Why):

Step 1: Collect defect data for 1 week।

Step 2: Pareto chart: Bar chart sorted highest to lowest defect type।

Result: Top 20% defect types usually = 80% of total defects।

Step 3: Focus on top 2-3 defect types।

Step 4: 5-Why for each।

Step 5: Corrective action।

Step 6: Measure DHU improvement।

Defect Tracker Format:

Date | Operation | Defect Type | Count | Cause | Action | Resp. | Status

Weekly DHU trend → improving? plateauing? worsening?

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
Quality = built-in, not inspected-in. Multi-level QC: Pre-production → Inline → End-line → Final → Pre-shipment. AQL system: Statistical lot acceptance. Defect classification: Critical, Major, Minor. Root cause analysis: Pareto + 5-Why. Target DHU <5.

PART XI — Production Planning & Control
अध्याय 19

PPC: Production का Brain

PPC: Production का Brain

Plan → Execute → Monitor → Correct — यही PPC है

19.1 — PPC Department: Role और Structure

PPC = Production Planning and Control।

PPC department factory का "brain" है — यह decide करता है क्या, कब, कैसे और कितना।

PPC के 5 Core Functions:

1. ORDER PLANNING: Customer order मिलते ही — style analysis, capacity check, delivery feasibility।

2. MATERIAL PLANNING: BOM से fabric, trims, accessories की quantity और timing।

3. PRODUCTION SCHEDULING: Cutting date, sewing start, finishing, QC, packing, dispatch — सब timeline में।

4. PROGRESS MONITORING: Daily production vs plan। Gap identify। Corrective action।

5. DELIVERY COORDINATION: Packing, QC clearing, logistics, actual dispatch।

PPC की Structure (Medium Factory):

PPC Manager

├── Planning Officer (order + scheduling)

├── Material Control Officer (MRP + procurement follow-up)

└── Production Monitor (daily tracking + reporting)

Small unit में: Owner या senior supervisor यह functions handle करे।

PPC के बिना Factory:

Orders overlap → capacity crisis।

Material not ready → production halt।

Bottlenecks not anticipated → delivery miss।

Customer anger → order cancellation।

Cash flow disrupted।

PPC के साथ:

Every order has a plan।

Materials arrive on time।

Production flows smoothly।

Delivery usually on time।

Customer confidence।

19.2 — Capacity Planning: हो सकता है या नहीं?

किसी भी order accept करने से पहले capacity check करें।

Capacity Calculation:

Daily Capacity = Number of operators × Working hours per shift × Efficiency% ÷ SMV (per piece)

Example:

20 operators, 8 hours, 75% efficiency, SMV = 20 min/shirt

Daily Capacity = 20 × 480 × 0.75 ÷ 20 = 360 shirts/day

Monthly Capacity = Daily Capacity × Working days

= 360 × 26 = 9,360 shirts/month

Now you know your capacity।

Order Feasibility Check:

New Order: 5000 shirts in 15 working days।

Required per day: 5000 ÷ 15 = 334 shirts/day।

Available capacity: 360 shirts/day।

Assessment: FEASIBLE (334 < 360) + 2 days buffer।

Another Order: 7000 shirts in 15 days।

Required per day: 7000 ÷ 15 = 467 shirts/day।

Available capacity: 360/day।

Gap: 107 shirts/day SHORTFALL।

Options:

A: Overtime — extend working hours। Extra cost।

B: Extra shift। Extra workers।

C: Negotiate delivery date extension।

D: Partial order — accept 5400 in 15 days।

E: Reject order (better than promise and fail)।

Multi-Order Capacity Planning:

When multiple orders running:

Order A: 2000 shirts, 10 days → 200/day required।

Order B: 1500 trousers, 8 days → 188/day required।

SMV shirt = 20 min, SMV trouser = 25 min।

Total capacity minutes needed: (200×20) + (188×25) = 4000 + 4700 = 8700 min/day।

Available: 20 operators × 480 min × 75% = 7200 min/day।

OVERLOADED by 1500 min/day।

Solution: Stagger orders, extend one delivery।

19.3 — Master Production Schedule

Master Production Schedule (MPS) = 4-12 week horizon का production plan।

MPS Format:

Week → Style → Customer → Quantity → Cutting Plan → Sewing Plan → Delivery

Example MPS:

Week | Style | Customer | Qty | Cut By | Sew By | Dispatch

W1 | School Shirt | ABC School | 300 | 01-Dec | 08-Dec | 12-Dec

W1 | Ladies Suit | Retailer X | 150 | 02-Dec | 10-Dec | 14-Dec

W2 | T-Shirt | Online Brand | 500 | 08-Dec | 18-Dec | 22-Dec

W3 | Uniform | Hospital Y | 200 | 15-Dec | 23-Dec | 27-Dec

Capacity Load on MPS:

W1: 300 shirts + 150 suits → check capacity each day of W1।

If overloaded: Adjust schedule।

Gantt Chart:

Visual timeline tool।

Activity | Dec 1 | Dec 2 | Dec 3 | Dec 4 | ... | Dec 12

Fabric Procurement | ████ | ███ |

Fabric Inspection | | ██ | ██ |

Cutting | | | | ████ | ██ |

Sewing | | | | | ████ | ████ | ██

Finishing/QC | | | | | | | ████ | ██

Packing | | | | | | | | | ██

Dispatch | | | | | | | | | | █

This visual helps identify overlaps and gaps।

Daily Production Monitor:

Every day: Actual vs target।

Line | Target | 10am | 12pm | 2pm | 4pm | EOD Actual | %Ach | Remarks

Line 1 | 200 | 52 | 95 | 148 | 192 | 198 | 99% | -2 operator absent

Line 2 | 150 | 40 | 82 | 118 | 152 | 152 | 101% | On track

Corrective Action Protocol:

<80% achievement by 12pm → Supervisor investigate।

<70% → Production Manager involved।

<60% → Corrective action (OT, resource reallocation)।

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
PPC = factory's brain. Functions: Order planning, MRP, Scheduling, Monitoring, Dispatch coordination. Capacity planning before accepting order. Master Production Schedule: 4-12 week horizon. Daily production monitoring with corrective action protocol.

PART XII — Material & Inventory Management
अध्याय 20

Complete Inventory Management

Complete Inventory Management

Inventory = Blocked Cash; Manage it to release cash

20.1 — Inventory Management Philosophy

Inventory = किसी भी material जो factory में है — raw, WIP, या finished।

इसे "asset" भी कहते हैं — पर यह "blocked cash" भी है।

₹5 Lakh का inventory = ₹5 Lakh cash जो work नहीं कर रहा।

Inventory Management का Goal:

Right material → Right quantity → Right place → Right time → Right cost।

Too much inventory:

• Cash blocked।

• Storage cost।

• Risk of damage, obsolescence।

• Hidden quality issues।

• Masks production problems।

Too little inventory:

• Production stoppages।

• Rush purchases (expensive)।

• Delivery delays।

Optimal inventory = Just enough — not more, not less।

Inventory Categories in Garment Factory:

Raw Material:

• Main fabric (most valuable)।

• Trims (buttons, zippers, elastic, lace)।

• Thread।

• Labels, tags।

• Packaging material।

Work in Progress (WIP):

• Fabric cut but not sewn।

• In sewing (partially sewn)।

• Sewn but not finished।

• Finished but not packed।

Finished Goods:

• Packed, ready for dispatch।

• Shipped but not invoiced।

20.2 — Stock Control Systems

ABC Analysis for Inventory:

A Items: High value, critical। (Fabric, specialty trims)

• Tight control। Daily monitoring। Small safety stock।

B Items: Medium value। (Buttons, thread)

• Weekly monitoring। Moderate safety stock।

C Items: Low value, many types। (Needles, poly bags, tags)

• Monthly monitoring। Higher safety stock (bulk buy — cheaper)।

Inventory Formula:

Safety Stock = Average daily consumption × Lead time + Safety buffer।

Reorder Point = Safety Stock + (Average daily consumption × Lead time)।

Example:

Fabric consumption: 50 m/day।

Lead time: 7 days।

Safety buffer: 3 days consumption = 150 m।

Safety Stock = (50 × 7) + 150 = 350 + 150 = 500 m।

Reorder Point = 500 + (50 × 7) = 500 + 350 = 850 m।

• When stock reaches 850 m: ORDER immediately।

EOQ (Economic Order Quantity):

EOQ = √(2 × Annual Demand × Ordering Cost ÷ Holding Cost/unit)

Simplified: Balance between ordering frequently (higher ordering cost) vs ordering large quantities (higher holding cost)।

FIFO (First In First Out):

First received → First used।

Prevents fabric aging, color fading, yellowing।

Especially important for white/light fabrics।

Implementation: Label every roll with date received। Use oldest rolls first।

Shade Control:

Never mix fabric from different shade batches in one garment।

Shade number on every roll।

Numbering system in cutting।

20.3 — Store Management

Physical Store Organization:

Zone System:

Zone A: Active production materials (currently in production order)।

Zone B: Next order materials (received, waiting)।

Zone C: Safety stock।

Zone D: Returns and claims।

Within each zone:

Style/Order wise separation।

FIFO arrangement।

Clear labeling।

Fabric Store Specifics:

Roll stacking: Maximum 3-4 high (safety)। Horizontal preferred।

Weight limit per shelf: Check shelf capacity।

Climate: Avoid direct sunlight। Humidity control for sensitive fabrics।

Pest control: Monthly।

Trims Store:

Component-wise storage (all buttons together, all zippers)।

Sub-divide by size, color, type।

Minimum quantity label visible।

Count quarterly।

Issue Control:

Issue only against Production Order।

BOM-based quantities।

Over-issue: Document reason, supervisor approval।

Issue Slip: Material, quantity, order number, date, recipient signature।

Physical Stock Taking:

Monthly: Random spot check (20-30% of items)।

Quarterly: Full physical stock count।

Annual: Complete audit।

Variance: Physical vs book — investigate।

Acceptable variance: ±2% for fabric (natural wastage)। Zero for costly trims।

Dead Stock Action:

3 months no movement: Review।

6 months no movement: Mark as dead stock।

Action: Return to supplier, sell to scrap dealer, use in sample, donate।

Dead stock cost: Storage + value erosion + opportunity cost।

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
Inventory = blocked cash. ABC analysis for control priorities. Safety stock, reorder point calculations. FIFO prevents fabric aging. Store zoning and organization. Monthly spot checks, quarterly full count. Dead stock = take action, don't ignore.

PART XIII — Costing & Business Economics
अध्याय 21

Garment Costing का Complete Framework

Garment Costing का Complete Framework

Numbers tell the truth — costing is your business compass

21.1 — Why Costing Matters: Real Stories

Story 1: The Successful-Looking Failure

Ramesh's garment unit was always busy. Orders coming in, machines running, workers productive. But at year end — bank balance same as beginning. Why?

Analysis: His pricing was based on competitor prices, not his costs. He was selling at ₹250/piece while his actual cost was ₹265/piece.

Running a business with negative margin — only possible while capital depletes.

Solution: Accurate costing. Repricing. Some orders refused. Profitability achieved.

Story 2: The Order That Destroyed the Business

Priya got a large institutional order — 3000 pieces. Exciting. She accepted at ₹180/piece (competitor's price). Started production. Mid-way realized fabric cost alone was ₹140/piece, labour ₹35, overhead ₹20 → total ₹195/piece. Selling at ₹180.

Loss: ₹45/piece × 3000 = ₹1,35,000 loss on the order.

Plus: Cash flow crisis during production. Workers delayed payment.

Lesson: ALWAYS calculate cost BEFORE accepting order. NEVER price based on competition without first knowing your own cost.

Costing Formula:

Total Cost = Direct Material + Direct Labour + Manufacturing Overhead + Selling & Distribution Cost

Selling Price = Total Cost + Desired Profit Margin

Minimum Selling Price = Total Cost + Minimum Margin (break even above zero)।

21.2 — Complete Costing Sheet: Men's Formal Shirt

Let's build a complete costing sheet step by step.

PRODUCT: Men's Formal Shirt (Standard, 100% Cotton Poplin)

SIZE: M (chest 40")

QUANTITY: 1 piece (for per piece cost)

SECTION A: DIRECT MATERIAL COST

A1. Main Fabric:

Body fabric (Poplin, 45" wide): 1.65 m per shirt

Price: ₹90/m

Cost: 1.65 × ₹90 = ₹148.50

Wastage @8%: ₹11.88

A1 Total: ₹160.38

A2. Interlining/Fusing:

Collar: 0.06 m × ₹120/m = ₹7.20

Collar Stand: 0.04 m × ₹120/m = ₹4.80

Cuff (2): 0.10 m × ₹120/m = ₹12.00

Placket: 0.06 m × ₹120/m = ₹7.20

A2 Total: ₹31.20

A3. Thread:

Main sewing: 80 m

Buttonhole: 10 m

Button attach: 5 m

Total: 95 m thread (mixed)

Cost: ₹0.06/m × 95 = ₹5.70

A3 Total: ₹5.70

A4. Buttons:

Front: 6 nos × ₹2 = ₹12.00

Cuff: 4 nos × ₹2 = ₹8.00

Collar: 1 nos × ₹2 = ₹2.00

Spare: 1 nos × ₹2 = ₹2.00

A4 Total: ₹24.00

A5. Labels:

Brand label: ₹3.00

Size label: ₹1.50

Care label: ₹1.50

A5 Total: ₹6.00

A6. Accessories:

Safety pins: ₹0.50

Collar stiffeners (2): ₹2.00

Tissue paper: ₹1.00

A6 Total: ₹3.50

TOTAL DIRECT MATERIAL (A): ₹230.78

SECTION B: DIRECT LABOUR COST

Total garment SMV: 22 minutes

Operator efficiency: 75%

Effective SMV: 22 ÷ 0.75 = 29.3 minutes actual time

Operator wage: ₹400/day ÷ 480 min = ₹0.833/min

Labour cost: 29.3 min × ₹0.833 = ₹24.41

B1. Direct Sewing Labour: ₹24.41

B2. Cutting Labour (per piece): ₹5.00

B3. Finishing Labour: ₹3.00

B4. QC Labour: ₹1.50

TOTAL DIRECT LABOUR (B): ₹33.91

SECTION C: MANUFACTURING OVERHEAD (per piece)

Monthly overhead for 2000 shirts production:

Rent: ₹12,000 → ₹6.00/piece

Electricity: ₹5,000 → ₹2.50/piece

Machine Depreciation: ₹8,000 → ₹4.00/piece

Machine Maintenance: ₹2,000 → ₹1.00/piece

Supervisor Salary: ₹15,000 → ₹7.50/piece

Admin Staff: ₹8,000 → ₹4.00/piece

Water, misc: ₹2,000 → ₹1.00/piece

TOTAL OVERHEAD (C): ₹26.00/piece

SECTION D: PACKING COST

D1. Poly bag: ₹3.00

D2. Cardboard insert: ₹1.50

D3. Tag + safety pin: ₹2.00

D4. Barcode sticker: ₹0.50

D5. Carton share: ₹3.00

TOTAL PACKING (D): ₹10.00

SECTION E: SELLING COST

E1. Transport (estimate): ₹5.00/piece

E2. Sales commission (5%): calculated on selling price

E3. Bad debt provision (1%): calculated on selling price

COST OF PRODUCTION (A+B+C+D): ₹300.69

Add Selling Cost estimate (excluding %): ₹5.00

TOTAL COST: ₹305.69

DESIRED MARGIN (18%): ₹55.02 + provision for commission = ₹65.00 approx

FACTORY GATE PRICE: ₹370/piece

⚠️ These are indicative numbers. Verify all costs with actual local rates.

21.3 — Break-Even Analysis और Profit Planning

Break-Even Analysis tells you: minimum production needed to cover all costs।

Break-Even Concepts:

Fixed Costs: Don't change with production level।

Rent, permanent staff salaries, machine depreciation, insurance।

Example: ₹52,000/month।

Variable Costs: Change directly with production।

Material, direct labour, packing।

Example: ₹245/shirt।

Contribution per unit = Selling Price - Variable Cost

= ₹370 - ₹245 = ₹125 per shirt।

Break-Even Point (Units) = Fixed Cost ÷ Contribution

= ₹52,000 ÷ ₹125 = 416 shirts/month।

Break-Even Revenue = 416 × ₹370 = ₹1,53,920/month।

Profit Calculation:

At 1000 shirts/month:

Revenue: 1000 × ₹370 = ₹3,70,000

Variable Cost: 1000 × ₹245 = ₹2,45,000

Contribution: ₹1,25,000

Fixed Cost: ₹52,000

PROFIT: ₹73,000/month।

At 2000 shirts/month:

Revenue: ₹7,40,000

Variable Cost: ₹4,90,000

Contribution: ₹2,50,000

Fixed Cost: ₹52,000

PROFIT: ₹1,98,000/month।

Sensitivity Analysis:

What if fabric price rises 10%?

Fabric cost per piece: +₹16 → Contribution falls to ₹109.

New Break-Even: ₹52,000 ÷ ₹109 = 477 shirts.

What if selling price drops 5%?

SP: ₹351.5 → Contribution: ₹106.5।

New Break-Even: ₹52,000 ÷ ₹106.5 = 488 shirts।

What if sales volume drops 30%?

1000 × 0.7 = 700 shirts।

Contribution: 700 × ₹125 = ₹87,500।

Fixed Cost: ₹52,000।

Profit: ₹35,500 (still profitable above break-even)।

Key Insight: High fixed cost businesses are risky — small volume drops hurt a lot।

Low fixed cost: More resilient।

For small units: Keep fixed costs LOW. Hire on variable basis when possible.

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
Costing: Direct Material + Labour + Overhead + Packing + Selling = Total Cost. Add margin = Selling Price. ALWAYS cost before accepting order. Break-even = Fixed Cost ÷ Contribution. Know your numbers — survival depends on it.

PART XIV — Human Resource & Skill System
अध्याय 22

People Management in Garment Factory

People Management in Garment Factory

Your people are your most important asset — invest wisely

22.1 — Garment Factory HR: Complete Picture

HR in garment factory encompasses: Hiring, Training, Managing, Retaining, Compensating and Complying।

Unique HR Challenges in Garment Industry:

1. Labour Intensive: 60-70% cost is labour. Small HR changes have big impact।

2. Skill Scarcity: Trained operators are hard to find।

3. High Turnover: Workers often move for ₹50-100/day increment।

4. Seasonal Fluctuation: Volume varies, but permanent staff must be maintained।

5. Compliance Complexity: Labour laws, minimum wages, ESI, PF।

HR Strategy for Small-Medium Factory:

Core Permanent Staff: Owner/Manager, supervisor, cutter, mechanic, key operators।

Flexible Workforce: Seasonal/contractual operators as volume demands।

This reduces fixed labour cost while maintaining capability।

Job Analysis — Before Hiring:

What work needs to be done? (Job analysis)

What skills are needed? (Job specification)

What will we pay? (Salary range)

How will we find candidates? (Recruitment channel)

Recruitment Sources:

ITI graduates: Good technical training।

Vocational training centres: NTC, NSDC।

Word of mouth: Existing workers recommend।

Local advertisements।

Walk-ins।

22.2 — Complete Role Definitions

OPERATOR (Sewing):

Responsibility: Assigned sewing operations — accurately, on quality, on target।

Skill Required: Machine operation, stitch quality, speed।

KPIs: Pieces per hour, DHU contribution, attendance।

Wage: Market rate + incentive।

HELPER:

Responsibility: Thread cutting, folding, material movement, feeding bundles।

Skill Required: Basic, trainable।

KPIs: Attendance, reliability।

Path: Train as operator।

CUTTER:

Responsibility: Spreading, cutting, bundling with accuracy।

Skill: Knife operation, pattern reading, counting।

KPIs: Cutting accuracy, wastage%, speed।

Special requirement: Physical strength, attention to detail।

QC CHECKER (Inline):

Responsibility: Inspect during production, identify defects early।

Skill: Specification reading, defect identification, measurement taking।

KPIs: Defect catch rate, escape rate (defects missed)।

Critical: Must be honest — not pressured by production।

SUPERVISOR / LINE LEADER:

Responsibility: Line management, daily target achievement, quality maintenance, attendance, training support।

Skill: People management, technical knowledge, planning।

KPIs: Line efficiency, DHU, OTD, absenteeism in their line।

Attitude: Must balance productivity and quality।

MECHANIC:

Responsibility: Machine maintenance and repair, downtime minimization।

Skill: Machine knowledge, repair expertise, electrical basics।

KPIs: Machine downtime%, PM schedule compliance।

Critical: Must not defer maintenance to continue production।

IE OFFICER:

Responsibility: Work study, SMV, line balancing, productivity analysis।

Skill: Analytical mind, time study technique, IE tools।

KPIs: Productivity improvement%, SMV accuracy।

Background: Industrial Engineering or Garment Technology।

PRODUCTION MANAGER:

Responsibility: Entire production floor — planning, execution, quality, delivery।

Skill: Planning, people management, IE knowledge, problem solving।

KPIs: OTD, efficiency, DHU, cost per piece।

Experience: 5+ years in garment production।

FACTORY MANAGER:

Responsibility: Factory operations + customer relationships + compliance + finance oversight।

Skill: Business acumen + production knowledge + people leadership।

KPIs: Revenue, profit, customer satisfaction, compliance।

22.3 — Labour Law Compliance

Labour compliance is non-negotiable in India। Non-compliance = penalties, shutdown, reputation damage।

Key Laws for Garment Factories:

1. FACTORIES ACT, 1948:

Applicable: Manufacturing premises with 10+ workers (power) or 20+ (without power)।

Registration: Factory must be registered।

Working hours: Max 9 hours/day, 48 hours/week।

Overtime: 2× wages for OT।

Women workers: Specific provisions for safety, working hours।

Annual leave: 1 day leave per 20 days worked।

Welfare: Canteen, first aid, creche (if 30+ women workers)।

2. MINIMUM WAGES ACT, 1948:

Wages: State-wise, category-wise (skilled, semi-skilled, unskilled)।

Review: Usually twice a year।

Payment: Monthly, timely।

Record: Wage register must be maintained।

⚠️ Check current state minimum wage notification। It changes।

3. ESI (EMPLOYEES' STATE INSURANCE ACT):

Applicable: 10+ employees, wages up to ₹21,000/month।

Employee contribution: 0.75% of wages।

Employer contribution: 3.25% of wages।

Benefits: Medical, sickness, maternity, disability।

Registration: Mandatory।

4. PF (EMPLOYEES' PROVIDENT FUND):

Applicable: 20+ employees।

Employee contribution: 12% of basic wages।

Employer contribution: 12% (EPF + EPS)।

Registration: Mandatory।

5. PAYMENT OF WAGES ACT:

Wages must be paid by 7th of following month (factories)।

No deductions without authorization।

6. MATERNITY BENEFIT ACT:

26 weeks paid maternity leave।

Cannot terminate pregnant employee।

Compliance Checklist:

• Factory Registration obtained

• State minimum wages being paid

• ESI registered and contributions deposited

• PF registered and contributions deposited

• Attendance and wage registers maintained

• Fire safety certificate obtained

• Electrical safety clearance

• Working hours within legal limits

⚠️ Labour laws change. Consult a qualified legal professional for current and accurate advice।

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
HR in garment = hiring, training, managing, retaining, complying. Each role has defined responsibilities and KPIs. Labour law compliance non-negotiable: Factories Act, Minimum Wages, ESI, PF, Maternity. Maintain all required records.

PART XV — Training System
अध्याय 23

Skill Development: Trainee से Expert तक

Skill Development: Trainee से Expert तक

Trained workforce = competitive advantage

23.1 — Training System Design

Why systematic training:

Without training system:

New worker starts, struggles, makes mistakes, feels demotivated, leaves।

You lost: Recruitment cost + training time + productivity loss।

With training system:

Structured onboarding। Clear progression path। Worker understands expectations। Builds confidence and speed। More likely to stay।

5-Level Competency Framework:

Level 0 — UNSKILLED (Day 1 to Week 4):

Cannot operate machine independently।

Learning: Machine parts, threading, safety, basic stitch।

Output: Not counted in production।

Supervisor: 100% supervision।

Level 1 — TRAINEE (Week 4 to Month 3):

Can operate 1-2 simple operations।

Efficiency: 40-55%।

Quality: Needs frequent checking।

Supervision: 50%।

Level 2 — OPERATOR (Month 3 to Year 1):

Handles 3-5 operations independently।

Efficiency: 65-75%।

Quality: Mostly self-managed।

Supervision: 25%।

Level 3 — SKILLED OPERATOR (Year 1-3):

Handles 5-10 operations। Can train others।

Efficiency: 75-85%।

Quality: High。

Supervision: Minimal।

Level 4 — MULTI-SKILLED OPERATOR (Year 2+):

Full garment capability।

Efficiency: 85-95%।

Can cover any workstation।

May become Line Leader।

Level 5 — SUPERVISOR/LINE LEADER:

Technical mastery + people management।

Oversees 10-20 workers।

Responsible for output and quality।

23.2 — OJT (On-the-Job Training) System

OJT = Training while actually doing the job।

Week 1: Orientation और Safety

Day 1-2:

• Factory tour — every department।

• Safety briefing — fire exits, first aid, machine safety।

• HR documents — attendance, salary process।

• Introduction to team।

Day 3-5:

• Machine parts — name and function।

• Threading — upper and lower।

• Basic stitch on scrap fabric।

• Safety practices: No loose clothing, tie hair, no reaching over needle।

Week 2-4: First Operation Training

Select: Easiest operation in line. Usually hemming, thread trimming, or simple attachment।

Training Method:

1. Demonstrate: Trainer does operation, explaining each step।

2. Guided practice: Trainee does it, trainer guides।

3. Independent practice: Trainee does it alone, trainer observes।

4. Speed building: Gradually increase speed as quality maintained।

Target Week 4: 50% of standard speed, 80% quality।

Month 2-3: Second Operation

Once first operation is stable: Add second।

Cross-training begins।

Speed builds on first operation — now getting to 65%।

Assessment (Monthly):

Skill Assessment Sheet:

Worker: ___ | Date: ___

Operation | Observed Time | Standard Time | % Efficiency | Quality Grade | Pass/Fail

Grade A: >85% efficiency + good quality।

Grade B: 70-85% efficiency।

Grade C: <70% efficiency — needs more training।

Trainer Qualities:

Patient, clear communicator।

Technically expert।

Encouraging — no shouting or demeaning।

Consistent — same standard every time।

23.3 — Multi-Skill Matrix और Career Path

Multi-Skill Matrix:

A visual chart showing each worker's proficiency in each operation।

Worker | Op1 Placket | Op2 Pocket | Op3 Shoulder | Op4 Collar | Op5 Sleeve | Op6 Buttonhole

Ravi | ★★★ | ★★★ | ★★ | ★ | ○ | ○

Sita | ★★ | ★★★ | ★★★ | ★★ | ★ | ○

Ram | ★ | ★★ | ★★★ | ★★★ | ★★ | ★

★★★ = Expert (>85% efficiency)

★★ = Capable (70-85%)

★ = Basic (<70%, can cover if needed)

○ = Not trained

Benefits of Multi-Skill Matrix:

Planning: Know exactly who can cover whom।

Flexibility: Absent worker → another covers।

Development: Gaps identified, training planned।

Fair incentive: More skills = more pay।

Career Path in Garment Factory:

Day 1: Unskilled/Helper

3 months: Operator (Level 1-2)

1 year: Skilled Operator (Level 3)

2-3 years: Multi-skilled Operator (Level 4)

3-5 years: Line Leader / Senior Operator

5+ years: Supervisor

7-10 years: Production Manager

Skill Incentive System:

Base wage: ₹400/day

+₹20/day for each additional operation certified (Level ★★★)

Maximum extra: ₹100/day (5 additional operations)

Senior Multi-skilled: ₹500/day

Line Leader: ₹600-700/day

Supervisor: ₹800-1000/day

This incentivizes learning and retains skilled workers।

Government Training Programmes:

PMKVY: Pradhan Mantri Kaushal Vikas Yojana — free skill training।

ASDP (Apparel, Made-ups and Home Furnishing): Sector-specific scheme।

NSDC approved training centres।

ITI courses in garment technology।

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
5-level competency: Unskilled → Trainee → Operator → Skilled → Multi-skilled → Supervisor. OJT: Demonstrate → Guided practice → Independent → Speed build. Monthly skill assessment. Multi-skill matrix for planning. Career path + skill incentive = retention.

PART XVI — Supply Chain
अध्याय 24

Supply Chain Management for Garment Units

Supply Chain Management for Garment Units

Supply chain strength = business resilience

24.1 — Supply Chain: Your Business Network

Supply Chain = All entities and activities that bring a product from raw material to end customer।

Garment Supply Chain Map:

Cotton Farm → Gin → Spinning Mill → Weaving/Knitting → Dyeing/Finishing → GARMENT FACTORY → Distribution → Retailer → Consumer

You (garment factory) are in the middle. You depend on everything before you। Your customer depends on everything after।

Supply Chain = Series of dependencies।

Your supply chain risks:

Upstream risks (before you):

• Fabric supplier delays delivery।

• Quality problem in fabric।

• Price increase।

• Supplier goes out of business।

Downstream risks (after you):

• Customer cancels order।

• Payment delayed।

• Return and rejection।

• Demand fluctuation।

Supply Chain Strategy:

Upstream: Multiple suppliers, quality standards, early orders।

Downstream: Multiple customers, diversified channels, advance payment negotiation।

Supply Chain Performance Metrics:

Lead Time: Time from order to delivery।

OTD (On-Time Delivery): % orders on time।

Fill Rate: % of ordered items fulfilled completely।

Quality Rate: % accepted by customer।

Cost of Supply Chain: Total procurement + logistics cost।

24.2 — Vendor Management

Vendor = Supplier of materials or services।

Vendor Selection Process:

Step 1: Define requirements। Quality standard, quantity, delivery frequency, credit terms।

Step 2: Research options। Market visit, trade shows, references।

Step 3: Sample evaluation। Request samples। Evaluate quality।

Step 4: Commercial discussion। Price, payment, MOQ।

Step 5: Trial order। Small first order।

Step 6: Performance evaluation। Quality, delivery, service।

Step 7: Approval or rejection।

Vendor Evaluation Criteria (Score 1-5 each):

Quality: Material meets specifications consistently।

Delivery: On time, right quantity।

Price: Competitive in market।

Communication: Responsive, proactive।

Financial Stability: Will not close suddenly।

Technical Support: Helps solve problems।

Capacity: Can scale with you।

Location: How far, how fast।

Vendor Scorecard (quarterly):

Vendor | Quality | Delivery | Price | Service | Total | Rating

A | 4.5 | 4.0 | 3.5 | 4.0 | 16.0 | A Grade

B | 3.0 | 3.5 | 4.5 | 3.0 | 14.0 | B Grade

C | 2.0 | 2.5 | 5.0 | 2.0 | 11.5 | C Grade (review/exit)

Vendor Relationship:

Adversarial (bad): Always negotiating price down। Vendor reduces quality। Both lose।

Partnership (good): Fair price, long-term relationship. Vendor prioritizes your orders। Both win।

"Good vendor relationships" pays:

• Priority during fabric shortage।

• Credit during cash crunch।

• Quality consistency over time।

• Technical help for new products।

Backup Vendor Policy:

For every critical material: Minimum 2 approved vendors।

Primary: 70-80% orders।

Secondary: 20-30% orders (maintain relationship)।

Emergency: Know where to go if both fail।

24.3 — Logistics और Lead Time

Logistics = Physical movement of materials — from vendor to factory, factory to customer।

Inbound Logistics (Vendor → Factory):

Local market: Same city। Own vehicle or hired tempo। 1-3 days।

Another city: Courier, transport company। 3-7 days।

Another state: Roadways, rail freight. 5-15 days।

Import: Air (fast, expensive) or Sea (slow, cheap). 15-45+ days।

Outbound Logistics (Factory → Customer):

Local institutional: Own delivery। Same day।

City retail: Weekly delivery rounds。

Pan-India: Courier (1-7 days depending on location)।

Export: Air cargo or Sea freight। 7-45 days।

Lead Time Calculation:

Total Lead Time = Material Lead Time + Production Lead Time + QC Time + Delivery Time

Example for Export Order:

Fabric lead time: 15 days (local mill)

Button lead time: 7 days

Production: 20 days

QC + Packing: 3 days

Sea freight: 20 days

Total: 65 days from order to delivery

This means: If buyer wants goods in 60 days, you cannot accept this order without already having fabric in stock।

Lead Time Reduction Strategies:

Pre-position safety stock for fast-moving materials।

Strong supplier relationships = priority processing।

Simplified approval process (fast PP approvals)।

Parallel activities: Cut before some approvals (at your risk)।

Air freight instead of sea (expensive but fast)।

Customs and Documentation (for import/export):

Import: Bill of Entry, Customs duty, GST, IGST।

Export: Shipping Bill, Bill of Lading, Certificate of Origin, GST refund।

⚠️ Customs rules and duties change. Verify current regulations from DGFT, Customs websites।

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
Supply chain = your business network. Upstream (suppliers) and downstream (customers) risks need management. Vendor selection: quality, delivery, price, service. Backup vendors for critical materials. Lead time = sum of all stages. Reduce through pre-positioning, strong relationships.

PART XVII — Export Garment Industry
अध्याय 25

Export: Opportunity और Process

Export: Opportunity और Process

Export — domestic से बड़ा market, higher standards

25.1 — Export Landscape: India's Position

India's Garment Export: Key facts (verify from AEPC for current data):

India is among the world's major garment exporters। Key strengths:

• Abundant cotton production और variety।

• Large skilled and semi-skilled workforce।

• Cost competitive labour (vs developed nations)।

• Strong textile base (yarn, fabric manufacturing)।

• Established manufacturing centres (Tiruppur, Bangalore, Delhi NCR)।

• Growing compliance capability।

Key Challenges:

• Competition from Bangladesh (lower labour cost, GSP benefits)।

• Competition from Vietnam, Cambodia।

• Compliance requirements (labor, environment, safety)।

• Infrastructure (ports, logistics)।

• High input costs (power, water)।

Government Export Support:

AEPC (Apparel Export Promotion Council): Industry body. Market development, trade fairs, buyer connections।

FIEO (Federation of Indian Export Organisations): Export facilitation।

DGFT: Policy and documentation framework।

PLI Scheme: Incentives for volume growth।

Trade Agreements: FTAs being negotiated — check current status।

25.2 — Export Process: Step-by-Step

Step 1: PREPARATION (Before First Export)

IEC (Import Export Code): Mandatory. Apply at DGFT online portal।

GST Registration: Mandatory।

Bank Account: Current account for export transactions।

AD Code: Authorized Dealer code at port।

RCMC: Registration-cum-Membership Certificate from AEPC।

Step 2: BUYER CONTACT AND INQUIRY

Sources: Trade fairs (India ITME, Texworld), AEPC, buying agents, online platforms।

Initial contact: Company profile, factory capability, certifications।

Buyer visit or virtual tour।

Step 3: SAMPLING PROCESS

Proto Sample: Initial design. Buyer reviews concept।

Fit Sample: Size set. Fit check on model।

Salesman Sample (SMS): Final samples for buyer showroom।

Pre-Production (PP) Sample: From approved fabric and trims. Final approval before bulk।

TOP (Top of Production): First production pieces — buyer approves before full run।

Shipment Sample: Retained from each shipment for reference।

Step 4: ORDER CONFIRMATION

Buyer issues Purchase Order (PO): Style, quantity, size breakdown, price, delivery।

Seller signs and accepts।

L/C (Letter of Credit) or payment terms established।

Step 5: PRODUCTION

Pre-production meeting: Buyer spec review with production team。

Material procurement।

Bulk production।

Inline quality monitoring।

Step 6: PRE-SHIPMENT INSPECTION

By buyer's representative or 3rd party (SGS, Bureau Veritas, Intertek, QIMA)।

AQL inspection on random sample।

Pass → proceed to shipping।

Fail → sort, rework, re-inspect।

Step 7: PACKING AND DOCUMENTATION

Packing: Per buyer packing instructions (fold, polybag, carton)।

Documents:

• Commercial Invoice

• Packing List

• Certificate of Origin (for preferential duty)

• Shipping Bill (Customs)

• Bill of Lading (Sea) / AWB (Air)

Step 8: CUSTOMS AND SHIPMENT

Export under LUT (Letter of Undertaking) → Zero GST payment।

OR: Pay IGST → Claim refund later।

Customs clearance → Port → Ship/Air → Destination।

Step 9: PAYMENT

L/C: Bank guarantees payment on document presentation।

TT (Telegraphic Transfer): Wire transfer।

DP (Documents against Payment): Documents released on payment।

Open Account: Risky for exporter.

25.3 — Export Costing और Compliance

Export Costing (FOB — Free On Board):

All costs up to the point goods are loaded on ship at Indian port।

FOB Cost = Production Cost + Inland Transport + Port Charges + CHA (Customs House Agent) + Export Packaging + Agent Commission

Example (1000 shirts):

Production Cost per shirt: ₹300

Inland transport: ₹8/shirt

Port charges: ₹5/shirt

CHA charges: ₹3/shirt

Export packaging: ₹5/shirt (extra carton marking etc)

Agent commission (3%): ₹9-10/shirt (on FOB value)

FOB price target: ~₹330/shirt

In USD (approximate): ₹330 ÷ 84 = ~$3.90/shirt FOB

Buyer may offer $5-6 FOB → margin exists।

Buyer offers $3.50 FOB → not viable।

Export Compliance:

Social Compliance (BSCI, SMETA, SA8000):

• No child labour।

• No forced labour।

• Minimum wages paid।

• Working hours legal।

• Safe working conditions।

• No discrimination।

Audits: Annual, by buyer-appointed agency।

Product Compliance:

• REACH (EU) — restricted substances।

• CPSC (USA) — safety standards।

• Flammability — specific requirements।

• Labelling — country of origin, care symbols।

Environmental Compliance:

• Effluent treatment (wet processing)।

• Chemical restrictions।

• Sustainability reporting requirements growing।

⚠️ Compliance requirements vary by buyer and destination country. Get specific requirements from buyer. Consult compliance experts।

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
Export needs: IEC, GST, AEPC registration. Process: Prep → Inquiry → Sampling → Order → Production → Inspection → Shipment → Payment. FOB costing includes all costs to port. Compliance (social, product, environmental) mandatory for export. Higher standards = higher prices.

PART XVIII — Technology & Automation
अध्याय 26

Digital Technology in Modern Garment Factory

Digital Technology in Modern Garment Factory

Technology = tool for efficiency, not magic solution

26.1 — Technology Adoption Framework

Technology in garment factory is a means, not an end।

Before adopting any technology, ask:

1. What problem does it solve?

2. What is the ROI?

3. Can my team use it?

4. What is the ongoing cost?

5. What happens if it fails?

Technology Adoption Stages for Garment Units:

Stage 1 — Basic (Small Unit):

WhatsApp for communication।

Excel for production and inventory tracking।

Mobile camera for quality documentation।

Basic barcode scanner (₹2,000-5,000)।

Tally for accounting।

Stage 2 — Intermediate (Medium Unit):

Cloud-based production tracking (Google Sheets, Airtable)।

CAD for pattern making (TUKAcad free, Gerber basic)।

Digital marker making।

Barcode for WIP tracking।

Basic ERP (light version)।

Stage 3 — Advanced (Large Unit):

Full ERP (Fast React, AIMS)।

Automatic cutting system।

Production monitoring boards (real-time)।

RFID tracking।

Digital quality management।

IoT sensors (machine utilization)।

Stage 4 — Future (Export/Premium):

AI quality inspection।

Demand forecasting AI।

Digital twin of factory।

Robotic sewing (limited applications)।

Key principle: Don't jump stages. Build capability at each level first.

26.2 — CAD और Digital Pattern Making

CAD (Computer-Aided Design) in Garments:

Pattern Making Software:

TUKAcad: Free version available। Good for startups। Windows-based।

Gerber Accumark: Industry standard। Expensive but comprehensive।

Lectra Modaris: Advanced features. Large factories।

Optitex: Good 3D visualization।

CLO 3D: Best 3D garment simulation।

Benefits of CAD vs Manual:

Speed: Grade a pattern in 5 minutes vs 2 hours manually।

Accuracy: Mathematical precision — no human measurement error।

Storage: Digital files — no physical space, no damage।

Modifications: Easy edits, version control।

Marker making: Software nests automatically — 2-5% better efficiency।

Digital Marker Making:

Software algorithms optimize pattern placement automatically।

Manual marker: Skilled person, 1-2 hours for complex style।

Automatic nesting: 10-15 minutes for same style।

Efficiency gain: Typically 2-5% over manual।

Impact of 2% fabric saving:

Monthly production: 2000 shirts।

Fabric per shirt: 1.6 m।

Total fabric: 3200 m।

2% saving: 64 m।

At ₹90/m: ₹5,760/month saving।

Annual: ₹69,120 saving।

CAD investment ₹1-2 Lakh justified by fabric savings alone।

3D Virtual Sampling:

Browzwear, CLO3D, Optitex 3D। Create digital garment on digital avatar।

Benefits:

• Reduce physical sample rounds।

• Fast design iteration।

• Reduce sample cost।

• Buyer can review digitally। Some large buyers now require 3D samples first।

Limitation: Not all buyers accept 3D. Physical sample still needed for most।

26.3 — AI और Automation: Realistic Assessment

AI and automation in garments — what's real, what's hype।

REAL TODAY (2025):

AI Quality Inspection:

Camera systems that detect defects automatically।

Companies: Uster (yarn), Oritain (fabric), various startups for garment।

Accuracy: 90-95% in controlled conditions।

Cost: High — large factories primarily।

ROI: Justified at high volume, high rejection cost।

Automatic Cutting (CNC):

Well-established technology।

Gerber, Lectra, Investronica।

Saves labour, improves accuracy, better marker utilization।

Cost: ₹20-60 Lakh।

Justified: 500+ pieces/day।

CAD and Marker Optimization:

Proven, widespread।

Clear ROI।

Accessible to medium factories।

Demand Forecasting (Retail):

Large brands use AI for demand prediction।

Factory level: Mostly for planning, not at small factory level yet।

ERP and Production Tracking:

Not AI but digital — well-proven।

Real ROI in production visibility and coordination।

PARTIAL REALITY:

Sewing Automation:

Status: Very limited for whole garment. Some operations automated।

Automated operations: Basic hemming (JUKI's auto-hem), pocket setting (pick and place), simple flat seams।

Reality: Full garment sewing automation still very difficult. Fabric is flexible — robotics struggle।

Timeline for widespread adoption: 10-20 years minimally।

Now: Human operators essential for complex garment sewing।

NOT YET REAL (for most factories):

Fully automated garment production: Not commercially available।

AI designing garments profitably: Still human-dependent।

Robot operators: Research stage।

Practical Digital Roadmap for Small Factory:

Month 1: Excel-based production tracking + WhatsApp coordination।

Month 3: Google Sheets with access for supervisor + owner।

Month 6: Simple barcode for finished goods।

Year 1: CAD for pattern making (if volume justifies)।

Year 2: Light ERP or production management software।

Year 3+: Based on scale and ROI.

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
Technology adoption: Staged approach. Stage 1 (small): Excel, WhatsApp, Tally. Stage 2 (medium): Cloud tools, CAD, basic ERP. Stage 3 (large): Full ERP, auto cutting, RFID. AI quality inspection real but expensive. Sewing automation still very limited — humans essential.

PART XIX — MSME से Industrial Factory तक
अध्याय 27

Three Complete Business Models

Three Complete Business Models

Your investment, your scale, your path

27.1 — Business Model Selection Framework

Before choosing a business model, assess:

YOUR CURRENT POSITION:

• What capital do I have/can arrange? ___

• What skills do I have? ___

• What market have I identified? ___

• What is my current production experience? ___

• Who can help me (mentors, partners)? ___

RISK ASSESSMENT:

• If this fails, what is my safety net?

• Can I afford to lose the investment?

• How long can I sustain without profit?

AMBITION ASSESSMENT:

• Is this a livelihood project or a scale business?

• Am I willing to invest time in systems and management?

• Do I want to employ people and manage them?

Match your answers to one of the three models.

General Principle:

Start with the model you can AFFORD and MANAGE।

Not with the model you ASPIRE to।

You can always grow from Model A to B to C।

Reverse journey (C to A after failure) is painful and avoidable.

27.2 — Model A: Micro Unit (1-5 Workers)

WHO IT'S FOR: Individual artisan, woman entrepreneur, part-time business, first-time।

INVESTMENT (Indicative — verify local prices):

Equipment:

SNLS machine (1): ₹20,000-30,000

Overlock machine (1): ₹20,000-30,000

Steam iron + board: ₹5,000-8,000

Cutting tools (scissors, scale, chalk): ₹2,000-3,000

Storage + table: ₹5,000-10,000

Total Equipment: ₹52,000-81,000

Setup:

Space deposit (if renting small space): ₹10,000-20,000

Initial raw material: ₹20,000-30,000

Working capital buffer: ₹15,000-20,000

Total Investment: ₹97,000 - ₹1,51,000

⚠️ Prices indicative। Verify locally। Second-hand machines possible at 50% cost।

SPACE: 150-300 sq ft. Can be home-based।

MANPOWER: 1-5 (including owner)।

PRODUCTS (recommended):

School uniforms (most stable)।

Ladies churidar-kameez (good demand)।

Children's wear (repeat purchase)।

Alterations and repairs (immediate cash)।

CAPACITY (Indicative):

Simple products: 5-10 pieces/day (owner + 2 workers)।

Monthly: 100-250 pieces।

MONTHLY P&L (Indicative Example):

Revenue (150 pieces × avg ₹300 margin): ₹45,000

Labour (2 workers × ₹10,000): ₹20,000

Rent: ₹3,000

Electricity: ₹1,000

Materials overhead: ₹2,000

Total Cost: ₹26,000

Net Income: ₹19,000/month

Note: This is illustrative। Actual depends on product, market, efficiency।

GROWTH PATH from Model A:

Year 1: Establish 3-5 regular institutional customers।

Year 2: Add 2-3 more workers।

Year 3: Move to Model B।

RISKS:

Single person dependency। Irregular income। No formal systems।

Mitigation: Build team early। Document processes।

27.3 — Model B: Small Unit (10-25 Workers)

WHO IT'S FOR: Entrepreneur with some experience, willing to invest seriously, wants systematic production।

INVESTMENT (Indicative):

Machinery:

SNLS (6 nos): ₹1,20,000-1,80,000

Overlock (2 nos): ₹60,000-1,00,000

Buttonhole (1): ₹60,000-1,20,000

Button Attach (1): ₹35,000-70,000

Iron + Press equipment: ₹30,000-50,000

Cutting equipment: ₹30,000-50,000

Total Machinery: ₹3,35,000-5,70,000

Infrastructure:

Space setup (rent deposit + renovation): ₹1,00,000-2,00,000

Furniture (tables, chairs, racks): ₹50,000-1,00,000

Electrical setup: ₹20,000-50,000

Fire safety: ₹10,000-20,000

Signage, misc: ₹10,000

Working Capital:

Raw material (2 months): ₹2,00,000-4,00,000

Labour advance: ₹50,000

Misc expenses: ₹30,000

TOTAL INVESTMENT: ₹8,05,000 - ₹14,50,000 → Approximate range: ₹8-15 Lakh

SPACE: 600-1500 sq ft।

MANPOWER: 10-25।

STRUCTURE:

Owner/Manager: 1

Supervisor: 1

Cutters: 1-2

Operators: 6-15

Helpers/Finishers: 2-5

Part-time mechanic: 1

PRODUCTS:

Institutional uniforms (primary — school, hospital, corporate)।

T-shirts (if knit capability)।

Basic ladies wear।

Contract manufacturing for local brands।

CAPACITY (Indicative):

School shirts: 150-400/day।

T-shirts: 300-800/day।

Ladies suits: 80-200/day।

MONTHLY P&L (Indicative — 200 uniforms/day):

Revenue: 200 × 26 × ₹350 = ₹18,20,000

Material: 200 × 26 × ₹200 = ₹10,40,000

Labour (18 workers × ₹12,000): ₹2,16,000

Overhead: ₹1,50,000

Total Cost: ₹14,06,000

Net Profit: ₹4,14,000/month (before tax)

⚠️ This is optimistic scenario at good capacity utilization. Actual results vary significantly.

KEY SUCCESS FACTORS:

Production system — not just machines।

Institutional customers — stable orders।

Quality system — to keep customers।

Financial discipline — costing accuracy।

27.4 — Model C: Industrial Unit (50+ Workers) और Government Support

WHO IT'S FOR: Experienced entrepreneur, serious investor, wants to build scale business।

INVESTMENT (Indicative — large range):

Machinery (50+ machines):

SNLS (25): ₹5,00,000-10,00,000

Overlock (8): ₹3,20,000-6,00,000

Special machines (buttonhole, button, bartack, etc.): ₹5,00,000-12,00,000

Cutting (straight knife, band knife): ₹2,00,000-5,00,000

Finishing (form press, tunnel): ₹3,00,000-10,00,000

Total Machinery: ₹18,20,000-43,00,000

Infrastructure:

Factory (lease + setup): ₹10,00,000-25,00,000

Fire, electrical, welfare compliance: ₹3,00,000-7,00,000

Working Capital:

3-6 months: ₹20,00,000-50,00,000

TOTAL: ₹50,00,000 - ₹1,25,00,000 → ₹50 Lakh to ₹1.25 Crore

SPACE: 4000-12,000 sq ft।

CAPACITY: 2000-10,000+ pieces/day (product dependent)।

MARKETS:

Export (direct or through buying house)।

National brands (private label)।

Large institutional contracts।

SYSTEMS REQUIRED (Non-negotiable):

• Full IE department

• PPC department

• QC system (AQL-based)

• Labour compliance (fully)

• Fire and building safety

• ERP or production management software

• Welfare facilities

GOVERNMENT SUPPORT SCHEMES (verify current status):

MUDRA Loan (Tarun category):

Up to ₹10 Lakh।

For small businesses।

Collateral generally not required at lower amounts।

Credit Guarantee Scheme (CGTMSE):

Collateral-free loans up to ₹2 Crore।

For MSMEs।

TUFS (Technology Upgradation Fund Scheme):

Subsidy on machines for textile sector।

Check current availability from Ministry of Textiles।

State Government Schemes:

Many states have specific schemes for textile/garment sector।

Subsidized land in industrial areas।

Power subsidy।

Interest subvention।

PM MITRA Parks:

Mega textile parks being developed।

Plug-and-play infrastructure।

Multiple states।

⚠️ All schemes have specific eligibility criteria which change. Verify from official government sources before planning.

───────────────────────────────────────────────────────

✅ अध्याय सारांश
Three models: Micro (₹1-1.5L, 1-5 workers), Small (₹8-15L, 10-25 workers), Industrial (₹50L+, 50+ workers). Match model to capital and capability. Government schemes available — verify current status. Start conservative, grow systematically.
